<!--
We're going to walk through overgeneralizations of four rough stages of TypeScript’s life: its public birth and baby years, the subsequent joyous hypergrowth, its cantankerous teenage years (happening as we speak), and some predictions for what TypeScript will be like as a young adult soon.
Throughout these eras, I’m going to highlight some high-level learnings relevant for those of us who aren’t developing a language.
-->

<!--
The early 2010s were a pivotal turning point for the web in general. JavaScript wasn't commonly seen as a "real" programming language. Rich websites were done with external systems. Flash in particular was a fantastic multimedia framework for vector and video graphics. Steve Jobs famously wrote an open letter in 2010 explaining why iPhones shouldn't and wouldn’t support Flash in 2010, and now Flash is a nostalgic memory. It was fully deprecated in 2017, depending on your support contracts.
“Macromedia Flash”: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adobe_Flash#/media/File:Macromedia_Flash_8_icon.png
“Steve Jobs”: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Steve_Jobs_Headshot_2010-CROP_(cropped_2).jpg
-->

<!--
JavaScript was not originally built for huge applications the way we use it today. But I do think it's actually a pretty lovely language - especially with the last decade of ECMAScript versions. It's flexible, fun, and comes with lots of syntactic sugar. 
Any untyped dynamic language gets much harder to work with at scale - whether that's JavaScript, Python, Ruby, or others.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

-->

<!--
Or if you were a developer in a more corporate environment at the time, your code might have looked more like this C#.
Microsoft needed a way to write its own web applications. And most of Microsoft’s developers were accustomed to writing code like this.
-->

<!--
The problem with ScriptSharp is that the web represents completely different paradigms of programming than traditional desktop or server applications. Existing compiled languages like C# add a lot of conceptual baggage that doesn’t make sense in a web-oriented world.
“Held back”: https://imgflip.com/memetemplate/193696283/Held-back
-->

<!--
TypeScript pared down its goals from ScriptSharp's and posited only two primary value props at launch:
1. Add type syntax and a type checker
2. Provide “future” language features for scaling projects
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }

<!--
This brings us to our first takeaway: understand your ecosystem. Don’t try to shoehorn in conflicting technology because you’ve preferred it in the past.
Learn the requirements of where you’re deploying code, factor in what you’re already comfortable with, and make a decision that will work long-term.
-->

-->

<!--
You can really see TypeScript's original marketing focus by visiting its oldest website snapshots on the wayback machine. Their phrasing was all about how TypeScript becomes plain JavaScript.
Mentions abound of adding in useful features like classes.
Lots of emphasis on how cross-browser and cross-OS TypeScript is, which makes sense given how distrusting the community was around Microsoft at the time.
What we don’t see on the website is a stated preference for one of programming paradigm. That's very important.
https://web.archive.org/web/20121002110941/https://www.typescriptlang.org/
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:148.86px;left:101.71px;top:195.57px;width:756.57px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Do what’s right, not what’s easy.</span></p></div>
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:78.68px;left:0px;top:0.59px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Learning #2</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
Our second learning is: do what’s right, not what’s easy.
The TypeScript team could have easily leaned into the practices of 2012-era Microsoft. They could have restricted it to Visual Studio and/or Windows, or shoehorned in C# sensibilities.
But they didn’t. They leaned into the web, keeping TypeScript open and workable for all.
-->

---
layout: none
hide: true
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

</div>

<!--
If you ever want a good time, search on TypeScript’s GitHub for all closed issues with the label “out of scope”, sorted by most comments. It’s a graveyard of broken dreams.
Some of my favorite front-page rejections are in there:
Runtime type checking: out of scope
Partial classes: in my opinion, having to write a class in one place is bad enough, let alone in multiple
Compile-time code macros: anybody else who just shuddered is a former C++ developer too
https://github.com/Microsoft/TypeScript/issues?q=is%3Aissue%20state%3Aclosed%20sort%3Acomments-desc%20label%3A%22Out%20of%20Scope%22
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:148.86px;left:0px;top:195.57px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:36px;font-weight:700;line-height:43.2px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:36px;font-weight:700">github.com</span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:36px;font-weight:700">/Microsoft/TypeScript/wiki/</span><br><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:36px;font-weight:700">TypeScript-Design-Goals</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
TypeScript’s design goals are resolutely stated in this wiki page. I’d encourage every enthusiast to read it. The list is a masterpiece of politely, but firmly, saying ‘no’.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:148.86px;left:70.5px;top:195.57px;width:819px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;line-height:37.8px;margin-top:10px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px">Non-Goals</span></p><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:28px;line-height:30.24px;margin-top:10px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:28px">1. Exactly mimic the design of existing languages.</span><br><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:28px">Instead, use the behavior of JavaScript and the intentions of program authors as a guide for what makes the most sense in the language.</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
My favorite of them is the first non-goal: TypeScript doesn’t try to mimic existing languages, like C# or Go. It instead leans into the existing conventions and intentions of JavaScript. Hell yeah.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:148.86px;left:101.71px;top:195.57px;width:756.57px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Play to your strengths.</span></p></div>
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:78.68px;left:0px;top:0.59px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Learning #3</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
Learning 3: play to your strengths. Runtime syntax is the realm of the JavaScript language. It would be a bad idea for TypeScript to add runtime syntax that might later conflict with future JavaScript syntax. Plus it's confusing to delineate what runtime code comes from which language.
TypeScript these days just sticks to types and type syntax.
Right?
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image33.gif" alt="Tim Robinson sarcastically asking &quot;YOU SURE ABOUT THAT?&quot;" style="height:361.14px;left:299.43px;top:89.43px;width:361.14px">
</div>

<!--
Well. We can't really say TypeScript has no runtime syntax.
-->

<!--
Three early features in particular did introduce runtime syntax not equivalent to existing JavaScript constructs:
enums: collections of related constants
decorators: Functions that can modify declared classes and functions
namespaces: originally called “internal modules”, a way to structure code as properties of named objects
(I’ll also note that TypeScript's classes ended up subtly differing from JavaScript’s classes, but that’s a story for another talk.)
It’s easy to look back and criticize bending-the-rules features as being too early, but they did serve a lot of value. TypeScript and many community projects heavily uses enums. Namespaces were a fantastic way to organize code before ES modules were formalized.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image34.png" alt="" style="height:536.4px;left:0px;top:1.8px;width:960px">
</div>

<!--
And decorators brought a lot of users to the language. Notably, Google had been building its own compile-to-web language, AtScript, for Angular 2.0. TypeScript’s early experimentalDecorators option allowed Google to instead embrace TypeScript for Angular 2.0.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:148.86px;left:101.71px;top:195.57px;width:756.57px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Don’t overreach.</span></p><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:21px;line-height:25.2px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:21px">(unless it’s really, really worth it)</span></p></div>
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:78.68px;left:0px;top:0.59px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Learning #4</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
I think the lesson here is that overreaching, or pushing past your design goals, is of debatable value. Not zero, not necessarily always or never worthwhile, but something you should think seriously on.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">

</div>

<!--
TypeScript hit 1.0 in April of 2014, released a v1.1 performance-oriented rewrite in October 2014, and skipped version 1.2.
The rest of the 1.x versions were all released in 2015, and contained a ridiculous number of features.
Start minute target: ~5
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }

</div>

<!--
Measure twice, cut once. Meaning: be very careful before you make a decision, so you don’t have to fix it later on.
Many of TypeScript’s 1.X features were things that, in theory, could have been implemented in 0.x when they were first ideated. But the language wasn’t ready for them then. Programming language features in a build on top of each other, and you need to solidify your core foundational features before moving onto more layers.
Carefully laying groundwork during TypeScript's 0.x years allowed that ridiculous 1.x year in 2015.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
TypeScript’s type system features follow a general theme of supporting common, necessary patterns already present in the JavaScript world.
My favorite example of this is Lodash’s get function: a utility that, in its simplest form, takes in an object and a key, then returns the value under that key.
-->

</div>

<!--
Well, in order to fully describe this three-line function, a programming language needs:
* Generics: allowing a type parameter for the container type, since the return type of the function changes based on the type of its input
* Self-referential generics: a type parameter for the key type, that's based on the container's type
* Keyof types: so we can constrain the key's type to that of the container
-->

Now imagine adding in more of Lodash's get() functionality, say how you can pass a key that’s a period-delineated list, or an array, to retrieve nested properties...
You can mostly model this in TypeScript today. It requires an accursed horde of various type system features to properly implement, but the Lodash types do it!
-->

<!--
The lesson here is to let your great engineers be great. It’s hard to explain how supporting recursive conditional types contributes to a company’s financial bottom line, but it’s good for the industry.
Every TypeScript enthusiast I’ve talked to has given much of the credit for TypeScript’s powerful and robust type system to Anders Hjelsberg, its principal architect. And for good reason. The man is a machine. Many of the awesome type system features we rely on came from him.
But Anders Hejlsberg isn’t the only important, influential developer working on TypeScript. There’s a whole team of people whose full-time role is to push the language forward.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:78.68px;left:0px;top:0.59px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Learning #7</span></p></div>
<div v-click="1" class="pptx-group"><img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image40.png" alt="" style="height:544px;left:0px;top:-2px;width:960px"></div>
</div>

<!--
The TypeScript team around Anders is itself stacked. It's not just Anders plugging away, it's a whole group of awesome individuals.
When you have a great team of people, encourage them to grow each other. Let the team cook.
In fact, plenty of TypeScript’s features and fixes have come outside of the formal TypeScript team. Our fellow speaker Mateus has gotten hundreds of PRs into the language. Ridiculous.
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }

<!--
Stepping further away from TypeScript itself, let’s talk about DefinitelyTyped. DT is the repository containing “third-party” types: community-authored type definitions for packages that don’t provide their own.
DefinitelyTyped is a feat of engineering automation. Much of the PR flow is automated, including pinging individual package maintainers for reviews and noting when changes don’t have requisite test coverage.
The TypeScript team keeps a (monthly?) rotation of which engineers oversee DefinitelyTyped.
Protip: DefinitelyTyped is also a great repository to get your first open source contribution in.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

</div>

<!--
Much of TypeScript's important community work is done with integrating tools. My favorite example was the Babel integration released in Babel 7.
Prior to Babel 7, there wasn’t a single good, recommended way to compile TypeScript code with Babel – which at the time was the dominant transpiler for JavaScript. 
Nowadays we use babel/preset-typescript to get TS code running in Babel. That came out of a collaboration between the Babel and TypeScript teams.
https://devblogs.microsoft.com/typescript/typescript-and-babel-7/
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
No language is an island. Developers need integrations, and integrations don’t come without a community. Community is key.
All that type system strength in the world is nothing to a developer if their favorite build systems or frameworks don’t support TypeScript.
-->

<!--
The growth years of TypeScript had a lot of great points, and I’ve portrayed them as a lot of joy. But as very parent will happily tell you, raising your baby is not all sunshine and roses.
Welcome to the teenage years.
Now, this is an oversimplification: no programming language is perfect, and many of the pains we experience with TypeScript today have been there since the beginning. But it feels different pointing out yet-to-be-fixed shortcomings in an up-and-comer, compared to complaining about the ecosystem’s dominant language.
Starting minute target: ~10
-->

<!--
Nod if you've heard this complaint before: "I miss when web development was simple".
I have to admit, this post of mine is trolling engagement bait. You can still do these things, and it hurt me seeing so many devs agreeing that you couldn’t.
The problem is that our web apps are getting ever more complicated, and the tools we're using to build them are evolving to better support that increasing complexity.
-->

<!--
Performance is such a bottleneck that we’ve seen multiple serious attempts to create TypeScript –or something like it- in a native speed language.
Just listing a few high-profile ones:
1. TypeRunner: an attempt in C++, halted in 2022 or so
2. Stc: this time in Rust, halted in 2023 or so
3. Ezno: another attempt in Rust, this time for a TypeScript-like language, but still early stage
Hot take: I think these are all doomed endeavors. No project I’ve seen in the wild has nearly enough funding to recreate all of TypeScript -let alone keep up with new TypeScript versions. If this is going to happen, it needs to come from TypeScript itself.
https://github.com/marcj/TypeRunner
https://stc.dudy.dev
https://kaleidawave.github.io/posts/introducing-ezno
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">

<!--
But even ignoring performance, have you ever tried to debug some of those fancy types? Here's a type error from misusing an API in the popular Zod library. What a nightmare!
Many would say the type system is too complex for its own good.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image49.png" alt="" style="height:533.93px;left:0px;top:3.04px;width:960px">
</div>

<!--
Others would say the type system also isn’t complex enough. Users are continuously requesting features that TypeScript doesn’t provide. Some requests are reasonable and good. Others are wholly impossible, while others still would be nice but are so complex that they’re likely still years away from feasibly landing.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image50.gif" alt="Homer Simpson on a wrecking ball being hit between a giant rock and a bar called &quot;a hard place&quot;" style="height:249.84px;left:208.97px;top:145.08px;width:542.07px">
</div>

<!--
There you have it: TypeScript is too powerful, and not powerful enough.
Clearly, the community has conflicting needs, and even conflicting understandings of what TypeScript is meant to be.
“A rock and a hard place”: https://tenor.com/view/homer-simpsons-homer-simpson-caught-between-a-rock-and-a-hard-place-gif-3377621
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:148.86px;left:101.71px;top:195.57px;width:756.57px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Aggregate, distill, extract</span></p></div>
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:78.68px;left:0px;top:0.59px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Learning #9</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
Such conflict is natural in a tool's lifetime. This is getting into the area of devrel, developer relations. These problems are as much around community engagement as they are TypeScript features. 
Even when users are being rude and swearing at you online, it behooves any project to respect the three core steps of user research:
Aggregate: determine the general trends of user rage
Distill: Find the real pains causing the rage
Extract: Actionable improvements to address those pains
Good devrel isn't a substitute for product development, but it helps immensely when working with masses of users.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-group" data-shape="6"><img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image51.png" alt="samuel-johnson-duo.BM-uJHPY_ZBsIjU.webp (829×419)" style="height:419px;left:65.5px;top:60.5px;width:829px"><div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:66.64px;left:26.1px;top:311.99px;width:268.57px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Impact';font-size:49px;line-height:58.8px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Impact';font-size:49px">DOCS</span></p></div></div>
</div>

<!--
The first improvements many devrel initiatives take are around first-party documentation. For good reason! The website is most users’ entry-way into a product.
“Samuel Johnson reading”: https://www.joshuakgoldberg.com/blog/split-out-unrelated-changes/
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }

<div class="pptx-group" data-shape="8"><img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image52.jpeg" alt="" style="height:480px;left:0px;top:30px;width:960px"><div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:66.64px;left:514.93px;top:30px;width:340.92px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Impact';font-size:49px;line-height:58.8px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Impact';font-size:49px">FIX THE TYPES</span></p></div><div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:49.68px;left:41.95px;top:396.57px;width:421.29px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Impact';font-size:35px;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Impact';font-size:35px">LOL I JUST USE ANY</span></p></div></div>
</div>

<!--
Without centralized guidance, users instead glean information from assorted other sources online. That’s unreliable and breeds misconceptions from incorrect or outdated materials.
Misconceptions lead to disappointment.
Disappointment leads to anger.
Anger leads to uninformed hot takes on Twitter.
“Yoda”: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoda#/media/File:Yoda_Empire_Strikes_Back.png
-->

https://typescriptlang.org
-->

First-party docs are more than a handbook on your features. They’re how you inform the world of the right and wrong ways to use you. They help craft how the community explains your stuff – if for nothing else, to avoid harmful misconceptions.
-->

<!--
I’m personally also excited about TypeScript and other tools making use of the new Node.js module compile cache. 
Quick JavaScript engine primer: Node.js runs on V8, whichs operate in a Just in Time, or “JIT” model. When you ask it to run some JavaScript it will first quickly compile it into optimized bytecode, then later swap to a more optimized version based on runtime behavior.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
Node recently added a Module.enableCompileCache() API that modules can use to opt into V8 caching its JIT bytecode optimization. That means the JIT compile time is skipped for all but the first TypeScript run on a system. That's pretty nifty!
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
TypeScript 5.7 and up start around 2.5x faster thanks to compile caching.
Startup time isn’t the bottleneck for most TypeScript performance pains, but it’s nice to see it being improved. I’m hopeful that more future Node.js versions continue to trickle up optimizations like this into userland code.
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }

<!--
Nobody likes having two windows. So up popped projects like ts-node that transpile code behind-the-scenes. One command to run, very snazzy!
Note that these projects only transpile, not type-check. Transpiling is fast; type checking is slow.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape" style="height:102.18px;left:172.88px;top:218.91px;width:614.24px;background-color:rgb(8,12,14);border:1px solid rgb(66,99,142)"></div><div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:101.18px;left:173.38px;top:219.41px;width:613.24px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:14.4px;padding-left:14.4px;padding-right:14.4px;padding-top:14.4px"><p style="font-family:'Menlo';font-size:21px;font-weight:700;line-height:25.2px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:21px;font-weight:700">$ node --loader </span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:21px;font-weight:700">ts</span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:21px;font-weight:700">-node/</span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:21px;font-weight:700">esm </span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:21px;font-weight:700">index.ts</span></p><p style="font-size:21px;line-height:25.2px"><br></p><p style="font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px;line-height:20.4px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px">"Hello, world!"</span></p></div>

-->

---

<!--
Outside of Node.js, we have other runtimes, in particular Bun and Deno. They support running on TypeScript source files out-of-the-box. Which is really nice – no need to manage loaders, or install extra dependencies, or any of that.
Wouldn’t it be nice if Node.js had this too?
“Bun”: https://bun.sh
“Deno”: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deno_(software)#/media/File:Deno_Logo_2024.svg
-->

<!--
By the way, the reason it’s called type stripping is because that’s what happens internally. All types-only syntax is replaced with spaces. That enables source map locations to still work – the source files are the same length, and all runtime code stays in the same position. Clever!
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:78.68px;left:0px;top:0.59px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Learning #11</span></p></div>

<!--
I’m not sure if the learning here is to embrace the platform, or to ask the platform to embrace you. Either way, the core TypeScript principles of avoiding runtime syntax and just working with JavaScript are what made type stripping feasible. 
Working as a standard Node.js package, not some bizarre Microsoft-specific creature, allows tsc to benefit from optimizations like Node.js compile caching.
-->

<!--
TypeScript isn’t doesn't only interact with the platform: we’ve also seen an increasing amount of TypeScript support in community tooling. Some tools support a .ts config file natively, even in Node.js versions without stabilized type stripping. My preferred linter and tester, ESLint and Vitest, both do!
“ESLint”: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESLint#/media/File:ESLint_logo.svg
“Vitest”: https://vitest.dev
-->

<!--
Plus, if you want to take the performance hit of adding in type-checked linting -which is a whole talk in and of itself-, we can give you a suite of even more useful rules.
Typed linting is surprisingly powerful and useful, and even though I know half of you have no respect for the linter at all, I’m going to show you what I mean.
-->

</div>

<!--
Traditional linting looks only at syntax. It’s like formatters in that regard, but for totally different purpose. Formatters just format your code. Linters can point out likely bugs and bad practices.
In this common case example, the developer has misinterpreted TypeScript’s empty brackets type, thinking it’s for any “object”. But fun fact, it’s not – any non-null/undefined value satisfies that type. They probably meant to use the object keyword. That’s something you can flag for in syntax-level linting.
https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-empty-object-type
-->

<!--
Typed linting goes a step further and pulls in information using the type checker. It can look at a piece of code and understand what type it is.
For example, it can let you know that you’re using await on a value that isn’t a ”Thenable” such as a Promise – which is unnecessary.
https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-empty-object-type
-->

<!--
By the way, here’s something a little less useful. My SquiggleConf co-organizer Dimitri got Doom running in the TypeScript type system.
It’s hard to convey how ridiculous this accomplishment is. He built machine code garbage collection, memory compression, global value stack, dynamic dispatches, and generally a full WebAssembly runtime that took 12 days to render the first frame of Doom. What a maniac. Look at his happy face.
https://shop.squiggle.tools
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0mCsluv5FXA&ab_channel=MichiganTypeScript
-->

<!--
I know I’m super biased here, but I really think 2025 –maybe 2026- is going to be the year of the linter for TypeScript. For years, ESLint and typescript-eslint had a monopoly on typed linting. But every other mainstream linter is working on typed rules. Biome has an experimental no-floating-promises rule.
Just imagine the implications of a linter that’s built for TypeScript. Your editor squigglies will update in milliseconds, not seconds. You’ll never have to restart VS Code or your ESLint server again. What a world that would be.
-->
